## AI Research

### [The effects of caffeine consumption do not decay with a ~5 hour half-life](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/vefsxkGWkEMmDcZ7v/the-effects-of-caffeine-consumption-do-not-decay-with-a-5)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span>epistemic status:</span></b><span> confident in the overall picture, substantial quantitative uncertainty about the relative potency of caffeine and paraxanthine</span></p><p><b><span>tldr:</span></b><span> The effects of caffeine consumption last longer than many assume. Paraxanthine is sort of like caffeine that behaves the way many mistakenly believe caffeine behaves.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>You've probably heard that caffeine exerts its psychostimulatory effects by blocking adenosine receptors. That matches my understanding, having dug into this. I'd also guess that, insofar as you've thought about the duration of caffeine's effects, you've thought of them as decaying with a ~5 hour half-life. I used to think this, and every </span><a href="https://www.gkbrk.com/caffeine-halflife" rel="noreferrer"><span>effect</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.fatcalc.com/caffeine-calculator" rel="noreferrer"><span>duration</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://benclarkxyz.github.io/blog/coffee/index.html" rel="noreferrer"><span>calculator</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.jitterliss.com/caffeinehalflifecalculator" rel="noreferrer"><span>I've</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://michaelroneil.com/caffeine-half-life-calculator" rel="noreferrer"><span>seen</span></a><span> assumes it (even </span><a href="https://www.omnicalculator.com/food/coffee-kick" rel="noreferrer"><span>this fancy one</span></a><span> based on a </span><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5020365/pdf/aasm.39.10.1827.pdf?mod=article_inline" rel="noreferrer"><span>complicated model</span></a><span> that includes circadian effects). But this part is probably wrong.</span></p><p><span>Very little circulating caffeine is directly excreted.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefuhqpm27ed3m"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnuhqpm27ed3m">[1]</a></sup></span><span> Instead, it's converted (metabolized) into other similar molecules (primary metabolites), which themselves undergo further steps of metabolism (into secondary, tertiary, etc. metabolites) before reaching a form where they're efficiently excreted.</span></p><p><span>Importantly, the primary metabolites also block adenosine receptors. In particular, more than 80% of circulating caffeine is metabolized into paraxanthine, which has a comparable</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefyfmjxus39b"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnyfmjxus39b">[2]</a></sup></span><span> binding affinity at adenosine receptors to caffeine itself. Paraxanthine then has its own 3-5 hour half-life as it's metabolized into a handful of other things.</span></p><p><span>Since paraxanthine is by far the dominant primary metabolite, and its further metabolites are mostly either very short lived or have poor affinity for adenosine receptors, we can fruitfully use a simplified model of Caffeine ⟶ Paraxanthine ⟶ Elimination. The upshot is an effective concentration curve with a broader peak and slower decline -- about twice as long to reach half of peak effective concentration, assuming paraxanthine and caffeine are equipotent -- than that given by the simple elimination model. When I say effective concentration, I mean the concentration of caffeine that would be needed to produce the same effect.</span></p><p><span>Below is a simulator that models caffeine and paraxanthine metabolism following ingestion of 100mg caffeine or paraxanthine (</span><a href="https://bkorpan.github.io/caffeine-simulator/" rel="noreferrer"><span>link to full version</span></a><span>). Note that the relative potency of paraxanthine can be adjusted; I am uncertain about how it compares to caffeine within that 4-fold window (see the section on relative potency).</span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><h2><span>Paraxanthine supplements</span></h2><p><span>If the above model is correct, paraxanthine itself is sort of like caffeine that behaves the way I mistakenly used to believe caffeine behaves. Directly using paraxanthine as a stimulant would have two major differences/advantages compared to caffeine:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Effects wear off a lot faster, which means you can take it later in the day without affecting sleep as much (or in the morning without affecting a siesta as much)</span></li><li value="2"><span>For a given peak level of stimulation you probably develop less tolerance, since active molecules don't hang around as long (really just another implication of point 1)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefsi381btna0r"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnsi381btna0r">[3]</a></sup></span></li></ol><p><span>(It might also have some other differences, like a somewhat different profile of effects.)</span></p><p><span>In the US you can buy paraxanthine itself as a supplement (I believe it only became available recently, in 2022). I've been using </span><a href="https://www.vitaminshoppe.com/p/paraxanthine-capsules-n-a-100-vegetarian-capsules/vs-0440" rel="noreferrer"><span>100mg capsules</span></a><span> intermittently in the last few weeks.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefkxvmfucyvvk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnkxvmfucyvvk">[4]</a></sup></span><span> Some early impressions:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Taking one or two capsules in the morning has a similar wakefulness-promoting effect to drinking a cup or two of coffee in the morning</span></li><li value="2"><span>I've taken one capsule around 4-5pm a handful of times, which did not interfere with going to sleep at 10-11pm</span></li><li value="3"><span>Taking a capsule at 7pm gave me enough energy to do some work in the evening, and did not interfere with going to sleep at 11:30pm</span></li><li value="4"><span>Subjectively the peak effects of 100mg paraxanthine feel similar or weaker than those of 100mg caffeine, while 200mg paraxanthine feels stronger than 100mg caffeine</span></li><li value="5"><span>The effects seem to peak within an hour of dosing (maybe 30-45 minutes)</span></li><li value="6"><span>I sometimes feel a bit of an energy crash 2-3 hours after dosing when I take paraxanthine in the morning, but not in the afternoon/evening</span></li></ul><p><span>The supplements sold in the US appear to exclusively use enfinity branded paraxanthine, perhaps due to them holding some </span><a href="https://patents.google.com/patent/WO2021151094A1/en" rel="noreferrer"><span>very broad patents</span></a><span> on the use of paraxanthine as a supplement.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefb8bw847syw"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnb8bw847syw">[5]</a></sup></span><span> On </span><a href="https://enfinityenergy.com" rel="noreferrer"><span>their website</span></a><span> they emphasize that paraxanthine has a "cleaner" effect than caffeine, is supposedly safer, and has a somewhat shorter half-life that isn't affected by slow caffeine metabolism.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflu5p49437m8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlu5p49437m8">[6]</a></sup></span><span> The somewhat shorter half-life undersells this point: it's effectively much shorter due to the lack of active downstream metabolites. They also don't say anything about reduced tolerance on the main page (though it's mentioned in the </span><a href="https://enfinityenergy.com/faq/" rel="noreferrer"><span>FAQ</span></a><span>).</span></p><h2><span>Exactly how potent is paraxanthine compared to caffeine?</span></h2><p><span>By "how potent", I mean the binding affinity at adenosine receptors. The binding affinity (Ka) is equivalent to the odds ratio that a ligand will be bound to some particular receptor at some point in time, divided by the concentration of the ligand (since the odds ratio is proportional to ligand concentration).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdpv9ev2rt5l"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndpv9ev2rt5l">[7]</a></sup></span><span> If one substance has twice the affinity as another, you only need half as much to get the same receptor occupancy.</span></p><p><span>There are four subtypes of adenosine receptors: A1, A2A, A2B, A3. The psychostimulatory effects of caffeine are thought to be mediated by the blocking of A1 and A2A receptors, with A1 receptors probably being more important. I was surprised to learn that the affinities of caffeine and paraxanthine for human A1 and A2A receptors are not well established in the literature. Below are my takeaways from researching this:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Paraxanthine has about 1.5-2x the affinity of caffeine for A1 and A2A in most studies where both have been estimated, across multiple species</span></li><li value="2"><span>Paraxanthine's affinity for human A1 and A2A receptors has only been measured in </span><a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0006295200005700" rel="noreferrer"><span>one study</span></a><span> that I was able to find</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>This result concurs with paraxanthine having ~2x caffeine's affinity</span></li><li value="2"><span>This study used a different experimental method for the estimation (adenosine-cAMP dose-response curves) than most other studies (radioligand binding)</span></li><li value="3"><span>The human receptors were expressed by CHO cells (a cell line from hamsters that's easy to work with)</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><a href="https://www.guidetopharmacology.org/GRAC/LigandActivityRangeVisForward?ligandId=407" rel="noreferrer"><span>Human measurements</span></a><span> of caffeine's affinity for A1 and A2A range over a factor of 5 and 20 respectively</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefsjgdgjxn74c"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnsjgdgjxn74c">[8]</a></sup></span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ul><li value="1"><span>The 5x range for A1 does seem to reflect genuine reported measurements, while I'm less sure about the 20x range for A2A (e.g. maybe the most extreme values were misreported in that </span><a href="https://www.guidetopharmacology.org/GRAC/LigandActivityRangeVisForward?ligandId=407" rel="noreferrer"><span>giant linked table</span></a><span>)</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span>From the above we might tentatively guess that paraxanthine is 1.5-2x as potent a stimulant as caffeine, while noting that measurements seem to be all over the place. This is in contrast to my subjective experience so far, where an equal dose of paraxanthine feels similar or weaker than caffeine. Some hypotheses:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>My subjective impressions are unreliable</span></li><li value="2"><span>The relevant receptors behave differently in human brains than in the assay conditions used by these studies</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>The measurements being all over the place is suggestive of conditions making a big difference</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>The A1/A2A antagonism model is incomplete</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ul><li value="1"><span>E.g. my impression is due to the lack of other subjective effects of caffeine besides the primary stimulatory effects</span></li><li value="2"><span>E.g. caffeine has additional stimulatory effects mediated by non A1/A2A pathways</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span>Less absorbed paraxanthine reaches A1/A2A receptors in the brain for some reason</span></li><li value="5"><span>The bioavailability (absorption) is worse than that of caffeine</span></li><li value="6"><span>Paraxanthine's peak effects are reduced by slower absorption</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="7"><ul><li value="1"><span>I doubt this: subjectively effects seem to peak quickly (maybe 30-45 minutes after dosing), and wear off fairly quickly (a few hours)</span></li></ul></li><li value="7"><span>There's less paraxanthine in the capsules than claimed</span></li></ul><h2><span>Concluding thoughts</span></h2><p><span>I recommend trying paraxanthine as a stimulant. For me personally, two use cases stand out so far:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Taking a 100mg capsule in the late afternoon/evening can give me enough energy to work when I'd otherwise be too tired, without noticeably affecting my sleep</span></li><li value="2"><span>Taking 100-200mg after waking can maybe replace coffee for getting me going in the morning, probably with less resulting tolerance buildup</span></li></ol><p><span>Considering how widely used caffeine is, I was surprised to learn through independent research that paraxanthine considerably extends the duration of its effects, which almost no one seems to understand. I was also surprised by how poorly characterized the interactions of caffeine and paraxanthine with adenosine receptors seem to be. There's probably a </span><a href="https://equilibriabook.com" rel="noreferrer"><span>civilizational inadequacy</span></a><span> story here.</span></p><p><span>If such an important point about caffeine -- the one ubiquitous nootropic -- was unknown to even the nerds, that's some evidence towards there being other low hanging cognitive enhancement fruit. That is, we expect more low hanging fruit in this world than in the worlds where the nerds already knew about this.</span></p><p><span>Paraxanthine-based stimulants look to me like a pretty darn low-hanging fruit that took forever to be picked; science has known about </span><a href="https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-663-05268-5_18" rel="noreferrer"><span>caffeine metabolism</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11572908/" rel="noreferrer"><span>paraxanthine's adenosine receptor antagonism</span></a><span> since at least the early 1980s, yet the paraxanthine supplements only became available a few years ago.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdp7feqwmj94"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndp7feqwmj94">[9]</a></sup></span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnuhqpm27ed3m"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefuhqpm27ed3m">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Caffeine can easily cross between the brain and bloodstream. This means that, because of diffusion, the concentration in the brain (where psychostimulatory effects are mediated) closely tracks the concentration in the bloodstream. When I say "excreted" I mean the molecule is removed from circulation, such that its concentration in the bloodstream (and brain) is reduced. This is mostly done by the kidneys, with the molecules ending up in urine.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnyfmjxus39b"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefyfmjxus39b">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Numbers are surprisingly hard to pin down here; see the section on relative potency.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnsi381btna0r"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefsi381btna0r">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>My guess is that equilibrium tolerance is roughly a function of the (per day) area under the curve (AUC) of equilibrium effective concentration.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnkxvmfucyvvk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefkxvmfucyvvk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The label says not to exceed 300mg per day. My guess is this comes from the maximum recommended daily caffeine intake of 400mg, which is metabolized to about 300mg paraxanthine in vivo.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnb8bw847syw"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefb8bw847syw">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The </span><a href="https://patents.google.com/patent/WO2021151094A1/en" rel="noreferrer"><span>linked patent</span></a><span> seems to claim any use of paraxanthine as a dietary supplement with a dose in the range 2-800mg.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlu5p49437m8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflu5p49437m8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Might not there be slow paraxanthine metabolizers as well? I suppose enfinity isn't incentivized to ask this question.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndpv9ev2rt5l"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdpv9ev2rt5l">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Note that reported values are usually Ki/Kd, which is the reciprocal of this definition. The meaning of these values is the concentration needed for 50% receptor occupancy by the ligand (i.e. 1:1 odds ratio).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnsjgdgjxn74c"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefsjgdgjxn74c">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Why such big discrepancies? I don't know. One thought is that, since adenosine receptors are G-coupled protein receptors (GPCRs), it might not be reasonable to summarize a ligand's binding affinity with a single number. This is because GPCRs have two different states, and ligand binding affinities for the states can differ.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndp7feqwmj94"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdp7feqwmj94">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This is also evidence that some circumstances recently changed. For example: a regulatory change, or cheap mass production of paraxanthine being enabled by other tech developments.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/vefsxkGWkEMmDcZ7v/the-effects-of-caffeine-consumption-do-not-decay-with-a-5#comments">Discuss</a>

### [101 Humans of New York on the Risks of AI](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/BCiDwMbvq5JRNAwwt/101-humans-of-new-york-on-the-risks-of-ai)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Nobody has ever done an in person door to door survey about AI risks</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefg96b00dcu9g"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fng96b00dcu9g">[1]</a></sup></span><span>. What do people really think about AI? Like really? There have been some surveys on the risks from AI. But there’s a real difference between looking at numbers on page vs. the feeling of talking to our fellow humans.</span></p><p><span>I</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffd8ecpinj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfd8ecpinj">[2]</a></sup></span><span> asked 101 people what they thought about the impact of AI. Approximately half of the responses were from ringing doorbells, and half were from asking people out and about</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefr611hce6f4p"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnr611hce6f4p">[3]</a></sup></span><span>. Every single one of these was in person face to face. Around 10 respondents only spoke Spanish and were surveyed in Spanish</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefihd7tgck0x"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnihd7tgck0x">[4]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><p><a href="https://sladebyrd.com/people-on-ai"><b><span>Here are the results.</span></b></a><b><span> </span></b></p><p><span>The top level results are very strong when it comes to showing interest in regulating AI. Everything else will be reflections on the results/process and all the qualitative data I picked up from this process.</span></p><h2><span>Thoughts on some Specific Questions</span></h2><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/BCiDwMbvq5JRNAwwt/kqa7mz04xf6sjxf1g6qx" /><p><span>This question was the longest and most complex question. I basically have to read out all of the options since it's not on a simple scale. I decided to first split it on whether someone thinks superhuman AI should be developed at all, and then only give the more granular options if they do think it should be developed. This difference in methodology could explain the difference between my results and FLI results, but it seems unlikely because even still the vast majority of respondents who thought it should be developed still thought it should be developed with serious regulation. It’s possible the initial framing of should or should not be developed biased the responses for the subsets of “Should be developed”. Overall these results are extremely strong though. Of the 96 people who answered with an opinion, only 2 of them thought it should be with current regulations or as fast as possible.</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/BCiDwMbvq5JRNAwwt/lufumsixp0nn2adrn4to" /><p><span>This question was terrible for a couple reasons. First people were frequently confused the “limits on developing more powerful AI” clause is just brutal. This was frequently misunderstood and my model is basically people just remember the most recent thing, so they simply remembered “more powerful AI” and discarded the limits part. People would be like yeah I am super worried about AI I hate it I never use it and then answer “much less likely”. I eventually ended up repeating “limits on developing more powerful AI” two times to try to help, but I think this was a good lesson on you want the absolute simplest question construction possible. There is just huge variance in reading comprehension and limiting that as a confounding factor is really important. Similarly, I started with “controls on developing more powerful AI” and I had at least 1 person interpret that as the politicians getting control over the AIs themselves. I tried to be quite careful with my wording, and this is part of why I made the simplest explanation of superhuman AI I could think of for question 3, but even still the takeaway here is when designing surveys you want basically no words that alter meaning, especially if they are farther away in the sentence. This question was also interesting in that it also got confounded by just general anti-politician sentiment. Some people answered less likely on the grounds that you simply can’t trust politicians at all. I think likely if I were to do this again I would simplify and ask if they would support a law instead of support a politician</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefpyors7fkew"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnpyors7fkew">[5]</a></sup></span><span>. There has been very little published surveying on the salience of AI risk to electability, so I am still happy I included this question.</span></p><h2><span>Takeaways</span></h2><p><span>Very few people are excited about AI. Almost everyone is worried about superhuman</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefs1b7vkm6lw"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fns1b7vkm6lw">[6]</a></sup></span><span> AI becoming too powerful for humans to control. The vast majority support slower development, international treaties limiting AI, and are just generally worried. People are generally worried about AI, even some of the people who said they were more excited than concerned about AI still frequently expressed worries especially in the context of questions about superhuman AI. My vibes based read is many of the people who are more excited are more excited because they don’t think superhuman AI is actually possible, but then when given hypotheticals about superhuman AI they are still worried about it/don’t want it to be developed</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffkjc6eu6rc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfkjc6eu6rc">[7]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>I did a pretty good job of getting a sample that is relatively representative of the US along the axes of Ethnicity/Income/Education/Politics, however there is certainly bias from only getting responses in NY/NJ. That being said the responses are generally very indicative of AI risk being both salient and politically advantageous (at least when it comes to electability) for both D and R candidates to try to regulate further.</span></p><h2><span>Surveying is Inherently Biased</span></h2><p><span>I had never canvassed before, it is crazy how many ways there are for bias to sneak in.</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>The type of person who will respond to a surveyor at their door.</span></li><li value="2"><span>It’s protected to ring someone’s doorbell, it’s not as clear whether it is legally protected to enter an apartment building and knock on doors. I didn’t do any canvassing of apartment buildings. I was mostly okay with this because the US as a whole is way less dense than NYC so going to less dense neighborhoods is likely more representative.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Time of day, I tried going before 4pm once and you basically only get people who are above working age. When canvassing on a weekday I for the most part stuck with canvassing 3:30-7pm to try to get a mix of working aged and not working aged people. The best time of day is after 5pm because then you can start to select houses based on which ones visibly have lights on</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref81hgontofy"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn81hgontofy">[8]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></li><li value="4"><span>How I give options. I found that when I give the question and then list off the answers that frequently confuses</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefno1j6v37f1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnno1j6v37f1">[9]</a></sup></span><span> the person being surveyed, so for most of the questions the best flow is to ask the question, wait to see what they say and then give the 2/3 closest options and let them pick between them. This helps remove the bias of the ordering of the answers, but it does mean that there is bias in which options I chose in the moment to give to the person based on my understanding of their answer</span></li><li value="5"><span>Sometimes the people clearly don’t understand the questions. I get into this more when I reflect on the questions I picked, but this adds bias in that if someone says I hate AI and then answers a question in a way that is inconsistent then I have to choose to be like are you sure, about that let me help you understand this question. So, if I am more attuned to certain confusions or more likely to clarify for certain answers that adds another source of bias.</span></li><li value="6"><span>How I say the questions. Do I emphasize a certain answer. Some people just do not answer the question. They just sort of ramble and go off on a tangent, and then when I give them options they simply will not pick one. Do I use my judgement and pick the closest option or say don’t know. I usually picked the option that I thought was most representative of what they said, but this is a judgement call that I am making.</span></li><li value="7"><span>Some of the respondents had never heard of AI before, so how I described it</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefkeceebk3sqk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnkeceebk3sqk">[10]</a></sup></span><span> impacted their responses.</span></li><li value="8"><span>Does my bias leak out? I was generally quite careful to open with “I have a survey about the impact of AI on all of us”, instead of something like “on the risks" of AI. If the person being surveyed thinks I am angling to get a certain type of answer that will both change the chance they decide to take the survey and potentially change their answers if they are trying to make me happy. However some people wanted lots of information about who I am/why I am doing this before they would talk to me. My answers were very much along the lines of “I am an independent researcher surveying about AI. I think AI is very important and it’s important to know what people think about it. Nobody has ever done an in person door to door survey on AI before. I hope to publish this.” Even just the way questions are phrased and the types of questions being asked leaks information about my bias, and this is hard to account for.</span></li><li value="9"><span>What do I do with various clarifying questions, what do I do if someone stops answering questions halfway and starts trying to figure out what I think? At the end of the day this is in person and they can decide to do whatever they want. This also means how willing I am to stay has an impact on the data. If I give up on people who behave in weird or harder to interpret ways then I am under sampling that type of person.</span></li></ol><h2><span>People are Weird and Surprising</span></h2><p><span>The beauty of talking to people in person is you get tons of qualitative data to go along with each response. Here are some interesting things that happened:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Someone when talking about the potential for losing control of superhuman AI. Said something along the lines of “I believe in God. If God wills it then AI should have more control”.</span></li><li value="2"><span>I had someone firmly disagree with the concept of the questions being answerable. “Why does it matter whether I think AI should be developed, that’s like asking whether I want the grass to be pink. AI already exists just like crypto it will just keep existing.” “If 99% of people wanted no more AI it wouldn’t make a difference since it will always be developed by rogue actors</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnij8gau9y1l"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnij8gau9y1l">[11]</a></sup></span><span>.” This person was the more extreme version of a couple other people who for whatever reason simply can’t or won’t answer a hypothetical. This is an extremely hard person to survey and I mostly just ended up putting don’t know for the relevant responses.</span></li><li value="3"><span>There a couple people who were concerned about AI, but still against treaties or politicians who try to limit AI. The general sentiment was basically you can’t trust other countries and you can’t trust politicians. This is always interesting to me since it seems like it should imply they are just against any legislation or treaty regardless of what it is about.</span></li><li value="4"><span>A couple people explicitly talked about how they were worried for either their or their kids lives.</span></li><li value="5"><span>I had someone who knew google top level results used AI, and who mentioned ChatGPT, but then on the 3rd or 4th question went totally off the deep end. She talked about how she’s not worried about AI and I shouldn’t be either as long as I believe in the 12th dimension and above. Apparently humans are 12+ dimensional beings and everything that will happen has already happened in the 12th+ dimension, so AI has already been around for thousands of years and will continue to be around for thousands more years. Some people just have wild out there beliefs</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffno1p45tn3i"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfno1p45tn3i">[12]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></li><li value="6"><span>One of the doorbells I rang just so happened to be a CTO, that was fun. He was generally concerned, pro regulation, pro treaty, etc. It approximately seemed like the people who used AI the most and had the most knowledge were more concerned.</span></li></ol><h2><span>You Can Do This Too!</span></h2><p><span>I built the survey app as a PWA (progressive web app) that works offline anywhere. I would strongly recommend trying this out yourself if you care about AI and enjoy walking around outside. Doorbells can be kind of depressing so I wouldn’t suggest that for your first time, but if you go to a park on a nice day</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefo1la15fee9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fno1la15fee9">[13]</a></sup></span><span> it’s super easeful! Just walk up and ask whoever looks like they might be open to chat, the worst that happens is they say no! If you want it to be even more fun do it with</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefcz5s35vpm0i"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fncz5s35vpm0i">[14]</a></sup></span><span> a friend</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref1u2qgg0n5gf"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn1u2qgg0n5gf">[15]</a></sup></span><span>, now you’re walking around a park on beautiful day with someone you like talking to occasionally getting to hear wild takes from strangers on AI!</span></p><p><span>It’s easy for me to add you as a canvasser, all you have to do is:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>DM me with the username and optionally password</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8o5esf2f4ab"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8o5esf2f4ab">[16]</a></sup></span><span> that you want.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Once I let you know you’re setup you can login at https://peopleonai.com/survey with your credentials.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Read the get started guide</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflwirwxypa3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlwirwxypa3">[17]</a></sup></span><span> and (optionally) add the page to your home screen</span></li><li value="4"><span>Go survey some people!</span></li></ol><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fng96b00dcu9g"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefg96b00dcu9g">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>As far as Claude can tell at least.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfd8ecpinj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffd8ecpinj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>along with two friends of mine.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnr611hce6f4p"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefr611hce6f4p">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Primarily in parks, but also on the street.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnihd7tgck0x"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefihd7tgck0x">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Which is also, as far as I can tell, the first US based Spanish speaking survey results.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnpyors7fkew"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefpyors7fkew">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Although I’m not really sure since part of the hope in asking a question specifically voter preferences is that it’s more compelling to a politician taking on an issue if they know it will help them get elected instead of knowing that people support that type of law in general.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fns1b7vkm6lw"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefs1b7vkm6lw">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Defined as: “Some companies are trying to build superhuman AI that would be far smarter than any human at nearly everything.”</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfkjc6eu6rc"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffkjc6eu6rc">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>There were a couple people who didn’t answer the question on whether superhuman AI should be developed because they didn’t think it was possible and refused to engage with the hypothetical.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn81hgontofy"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref81hgontofy">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>But of course this does add a little bit of bias, there might be some sort of systematic bias between the type of person who turns lights on (or doesn’t fully block out their windows, etc) and the type of person who doesn’t.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnno1j6v37f1"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefno1j6v37f1">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Huge range of education, intelligence, etc among respondents</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnkeceebk3sqk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefkeceebk3sqk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>“A computer that does the thinking of humans, like chatgpt, self driving cars, siri, or alexa.” Self driving cars was frequently the thing that was most helpful/understandable. It’s also kind of scary that there are people who have never heard of AI before, but they are certainly still seeing AI generated content on social media etc.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnnij8gau9y1l"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefnij8gau9y1l">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>When I talked about how if AI was banned it would be developed far slower since it requires lots of capital and developers etc, he was like yeah that’s irrelevant.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfno1p45tn3i"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffno1p45tn3i">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>And frequently this is also paired with never actually answering a question or getting mad at me for asking them to specifically choose one of the options. This is a case where I really have to use my best judgement to figure out which answer is most representative of the wild things coming out of their mouth.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fno1la15fee9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefo1la15fee9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Ideally on a weekend, but even on weekdays there can be plenty of people around if it’s nice out.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fncz5s35vpm0i"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefcz5s35vpm0i">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Thoughts from friend 1: “I had a lot of fun going canvassing around Sunset Park today. I have canvassed for a political candidate before and that felt a lot more standardized than this. Consistently, as we were asking people in the park today about AI, they were surprised and intrigued by the questions. People have lots of opinions, and it seems like some of them jump at the opportunity to have their voice heard. I was particularly intrigued by some of the responses that we got. I know I live in a bubble of over-educated and technologically literate people, but it’s still surprising to talk to people who have never used AI, let alone never have even heard of what it means for something to be “artificially” intelligent. It was also a really great opportunity to be forced to practice my Spanish in a community where 90% of the people we spoke to did not seem to speak English.”</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn1u2qgg0n5gf"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref1u2qgg0n5gf">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Thoughts from friend 2: “There were responses to some questions that I expected would lead to certain responses on other questions but sometimes I was surprised by what I perceived as lack of cohesion in the logic of people’s answers. It was fun to walk around with you and hang out in a neighborhood I’m never in.”</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8o5esf2f4ab"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8o5esf2f4ab">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>If you don’t, I will auto-generate a password for you.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlwirwxypa3"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflwirwxypa3">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Inside the app, it’s very simple</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/BCiDwMbvq5JRNAwwt/101-humans-of-new-york-on-the-risks-of-ai#comments">Discuss</a>
